\displaystyle\leq\frac{|\mathscr{X}|^{4}}{d^{4n}}\sum_{\mathbf{X}\in(\mathscr{X}^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}})^{4}}\prod_{i=1}^{4}p_{\psi}(\mathbf{X}_{i})

(
p_{\psi}((\mathbf{X}\mathbf{R})_{i})\leq d^{-n}
)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathscr{X}}p_{\psi}(\mathbf{x})\right)^{4}.

(by
Lemma

16
)  ∎

We further obtain that
p_{\psi}
and
b_{\psi}
are supported on
\operatorname{Weyl}(|\psi\rangle)^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}}
, which is a generalisation of
[
GIKL23b
, Lemma 4.3 & Corollary 4.4]
.

Lemma 54
.

Let
|\psi\rangle\in(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n}
and its characteristic and skewed Bell distributions
p_{\psi}
and
b_{\psi}
. The support of
p_{\psi}
and
b_{\psi}
are contained in
\operatorname{Weyl}(|\psi\rangle)^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}}
and
(\operatorname{Weyl}(|\psi\rangle)^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}})^{\otimes 4}
, respectively.

Proof.

We show that the mass of
p_{\psi}
on
\operatorname{Weyl}(|\psi\rangle)^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}}
equals
1
.

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\operatorname{Weyl}(|\psi\rangle)^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}}}p_{\psi}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\frac{|\operatorname{Weyl}(|\psi\rangle)^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}}|}{d^{n}}\sum_{\mathbf{y}\in\operatorname{Weyl}(|\psi\rangle)}p_{\psi}(\mathbf{y})

(by
Lemma

16
)

\displaystyle=\frac{|\operatorname{Weyl}(|\psi\rangle)^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}}|}{d^{n}}\frac{|\operatorname{Weyl}(|\psi\rangle)|}{d^{n}}=1.

(
p_{\psi}(\mathbf{x})=d^{-n}
iff
\mathbf{x}\in\operatorname{Weyl}(|\psi\rangle)
)

Now, since
b_{\psi}(\mathbf{X})=\sum_{\mathbf{Y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n\times 4}}\prod_{i=1}^{4}p_{\psi}(\mathbf{X}_{i}+\mathbf{Y}_{i})p_{\psi}((\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{R})_{i})
and
p_{\psi}
is supported on
\operatorname{Weyl}(|\psi\rangle)^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}}
, which is a submodule, we must have that
b_{\psi}(\mathbf{X})\neq 0\iff\mathbf{X}_{i}+\mathbf{Y}_{i},(\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{R})_{i}\in\operatorname{Weyl}(|\psi\rangle)^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}}
, and thus
\mathbf{X}\in(\operatorname{Weyl}(|\psi\rangle)^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}})^{\otimes 4}
.
∎

We have previously seen that the characteristic function of a stabiliser state
|\mathcal{S}\rangle
is uniformly distributed on its associated Lagrangian submodule
\mathscr{M}
, i.e.,
p_{\mathcal{S}}(\mathbf{x})=d^{-n}
if
\mathbf{x}\in\mathscr{M}
and
p_{\mathcal{S}}(\mathbf{x})=0
otherwise (
Equation

2
). Such fact can be recovered from the above
Lemma

54
, since
\operatorname{Weyl}(|\mathcal{S}\rangle)^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}}=\mathscr{M}^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}}=\mathscr{M}
and
p_{\mathcal{S}}(\mathbf{x})=d^{-n}
if and only if
\mathbf{x}\in\operatorname{Weyl}(|\mathcal{S}\rangle)=\mathscr{M}
.

We now prove that a submodule whose symplectic complement has high
b_{\psi}
-mass must be isotropic. For such, we shall need the following fact.

Fact 55

(
[
GNW21
, Lemma 3.10]
)
.

Let
|\psi\rangle\in(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n}
and
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n}
such that
p_{\psi}(\mathbf{x}),p_{\psi}(\mathbf{y})\geq d^{-n}\big(1-\frac{1}{4d^{2}}\big)
. Then
[\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}]=0
.

Lemma 56
.

Let
\mathscr{X}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n}
be a submodule such that

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{X}\in(\mathscr{X}^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}})^{4}}b_{\psi}(\mathbf{X})>\left(1-\frac{1}{2d^{2}}\left(1-\frac{1}{p_{1}}\right)\left(1-\frac{1}{8d^{2}}\right)\right)^{4},

where
p_{1}
is the smallest prime that divides
d
. Then
\mathscr{X}
is isotropic.

Proof.

Consider the set
\mathscr{A}\triangleq\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathscr{X}:d^{n}p_{\psi}(\mathbf{x})\geq 1-\frac{1}{4d^{2}}\}
. According to
55
,
[\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}]=0
for all
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathscr{A}
. Therefore, the submodule
\langle\mathscr{A}\rangle
generated by the span of all elements in
\mathscr{A}
is isotropic. We now show that actually
\langle\mathscr{A}\rangle=\mathscr{X}
. For such, we assume by contradiction that
\langle\mathscr{A}\rangle\subset\mathscr{X}
is a proper submodule of
\mathscr{X}
. We then have that
|\mathscr{A}|\leq\frac{|\mathscr{X}|}{p_{1}}
. Hence

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{X}\in(\mathscr{X}^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}})^{4}}b_{\psi}(\mathbf{X})

\displaystyle=|\mathscr{X}^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}}|^{4}\sum_{\mathbf{X}\in\mathscr{X}^{4}}\prod_{i=1}^{4}p_{\psi}(\mathbf{X}_{i})p_{\psi}((\mathbf{X}\mathbf{R})_{i})

(by
Corollary

51
)

\displaystyle\leq|\mathscr{X}^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}}|^{4}\sum_{\mathbf{X}\in\mathscr{X}^{4}}\prod_{i=1}^{4}p_{\psi}(\mathbf{X}_{i})^{2}

(by Cauchy-Schwarz)

\displaystyle=|\mathscr{X}^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}}|^{4}\left(\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathscr{A}}p_{\psi}(\mathbf{x})^{2}+\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathscr{X}\setminus\mathscr{A}}p_{\psi}(\mathbf{x})^{2}\right)^{4}

\displaystyle\leq|\mathscr{X}^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}}|^{4}\left(\frac{|\mathscr{A}|}{d^{2n}}+\frac{|\mathscr{X}|-|\mathscr{A}|}{d^{2n}}\left(1-\frac{1}{4d^{2}}\right)^{2}\right)^{4}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|\mathscr{X}|^{4}|\mathscr{X}^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}}|^{4}}{d^{8n}}\left(\frac{1}{p_{1}}+\left(1-\frac{1}{p_{1}}\right)\left(1-\frac{1}{4d^{2}}\right)^{2}\right)^{4}

(
|\mathscr{A}|\leq\frac{|\mathscr{X}|}{p_{1}}
)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{p_{1}}+\left(1-\frac{1}{p_{1}}\right)\left(1-\frac{1}{4d^{2}}\right)^{2}\right)^{4},

(
|\mathscr{X}||\mathscr{X}^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}}|=d^{2n}
)

which contradicts the assumption of the lemma. Hence
\langle\mathscr{A}\rangle=\mathscr{X}
and so
\mathscr{X}
is isotropic.
∎

4
Stabiliser measures of Haar-random states

Haar random states shall be employed several times in the next few sections, so we collect here some concentration results concerning stabiliser measures of Haar random states. As an example, we show that the stabiliser size of a Haar random state
|\psi\rangle
is
1
with high probability, or, equivalently,
b_{\psi}
is supported on the entire space
\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n\times 4}
with high probability. To do so, we will make use of Lévy’s lemma
[
MS86
,
Led01
]
.

Fact 57

(Lévy’s lemma)
.

Let
f:\mathbb{S}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be a function defined on the
d
-dimensional hypersphere
\mathbb{S}^{d}
. Assume
f
is
K
-Lipschitz, meaning that
|f(\psi)-f(\phi)|\leq K\|\psi-\phi\|
. Then, for every
\epsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\operatorname*{\mathbb{P}}_{\psi\sim\mu_{\rm Haar}}[|f(\psi)-\mathbb{E}[f]|\geq\epsilon]\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{(d+1)\epsilon^{2}}{9\pi^{3}K^{2}}\right).

We will apply Lévy’s lemma to functions of the form
\langle\psi|\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}|\psi\rangle
, which are
2
-Lipschitz as shown next. The next result is basically
[
GIKL23c
, Lemma 20]
.

Lemma 58
.

For any Weyl operator
\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathscr{P}^{n}_{d}
, the function
f_{\mathbf{x}}:\mathbb{S}^{d^{n}}\to\mathbb{R}
defined as
f_{\mathbf{x}}(|\psi\rangle)=\langle\psi|\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}|\psi\rangle
is
2
-Lipschitz.

Proof.

\begin{aligned} |\langle\psi|\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}|\psi\rangle-\langle\phi|\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}|\phi\rangle|&=|\langle\psi|\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}|\psi\rangle-\langle\phi|\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}|\psi\rangle+\langle\phi|\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}|\psi\rangle-\langle\phi|\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}|\phi\rangle|\\
&\leq|(\langle\psi|-\langle\phi|)\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}|\psi\rangle|+|\langle\phi|\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}(|\psi\rangle-|\phi\rangle)|\\
&\leq\|\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}|\psi\rangle\|\||\psi\rangle-|\phi\rangle\|+\|\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}|\phi\rangle\|\||\psi\rangle-|\phi\rangle\|\\
&=2\||\psi\rangle-|\phi\rangle\|.\hskip 216.2411pt\qed\end{aligned}

Putting the above two results together, we show that all Weyl operators
\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}
for
\mathbf{x}\neq\mathbf{0}
have very low expectation under Haar random states.

Lemma 59
.

For any
\epsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\operatorname*{\mathbb{P}}_{|\psi\rangle\sim\mu_{\rm Haar}}\left[\exists\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}:|\langle\psi|\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}|\psi\rangle|\geq\epsilon\right]\leq 2d^{2n}\exp\left(-\frac{d^{n}\epsilon^{2}}{36\pi^{3}}\right).

Proof.

Consider the function
f_{\mathbf{x}}(|\psi\rangle)=\langle\psi|\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}|\psi\rangle
for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
, which is
2
-Lipschitz according to
Lemma

58
. Moreover,
\mathbb{E}[f_{\mathbf{x}}]=0
over the Haar measure because
d^{-1}
-fraction of the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}
are
\omega^{s}
,
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}
. The result thus follows from Lévy’s lemma and a union bound over all
d^{2n}
possible Weyl operators.
∎

As a consequence of the above result,
b_{\psi}
is supported on the entire space
\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n\times 4}
with high probability if
|\psi\rangle
is Haar random, or more precisely, that
b_{\psi}
has a bounded fraction of mass on maximal proper submodules of
\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n\times 4}
, which generalises
[
GIKL23b
, Lemma 4.7]
.

Lemma 60
.

Let
\epsilon>0
and let
|\psi\rangle\in(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n}
be a Haar random
n
-qudit state. Let
d=\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}p_{i}^{k_{i}}
be the prime decomposition of
d
. Then, for all maximal proper submodules
\mathscr{X}\subset\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n}
,
\sum_{\mathbf{X}\in\mathscr{X}^{4}}b_{\psi}(\mathbf{X})\leq\big(\frac{1}{p_{j}}+\epsilon^{4}\big)^{4}
with probability at least
1-2d^{2n}{\exp}\big({-}\frac{d^{n}\epsilon^{2}}{36\pi^{3}}\big)
, where the prime
p_{j}|d
is such that
\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n}/\mathscr{X}\cong\mathbb{Z}/p_{j}\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

Consider any maximal proper submodule
\mathscr{X}\subset\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n}
. Then
\mathscr{X}^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}}\cong\mathbb{Z}/p_{j}\mathbb{Z}
must be simple for some
p_{j}|d
. By
Corollary

51
,

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{X}\in\mathscr{X}^{4}}b_{\psi}(\mathbf{X})

\displaystyle=\frac{d^{8n}}{p_{j}^{4}}\sum_{\mathbf{X}\in(\mathscr{X}^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}})^{4}}\prod_{i=1}^{4}p_{\psi}(\mathbf{X}_{i})p_{\psi}((\mathbf{X}\mathbf{R})_{i})=\frac{1}{p_{j}^{4}}\sum_{\mathbf{X}\in(\mathscr{X}^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}})^{4}}\prod_{i=1}^{4}|\langle\psi|\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{X}_{i}}|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|\mathcal{W}_{(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{R})_{i}}|\psi\rangle|^{2}.

Note that, in the above expression, there are
(p_{j}-1)^{4}\binom{4}{0}
terms of the form
\prod_{i=1}^{8}|\langle\psi|\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}|\psi\rangle|^{2}
,
(p_{j}-1)^{3}\binom{4}{1}
terms of the form
\prod_{i=1}^{6}|\langle\psi|\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}|\psi\rangle|^{2}
,
(p_{j}-1)^{2}\binom{4}{2}
terms of the form
\prod_{i=1}^{4}|\langle\psi|\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}|\psi\rangle|^{2}
, and
(p_{j}-1)\binom{4}{3}
terms of the form
\prod_{i=1}^{2}|\langle\psi|\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}|\psi\rangle|^{2}
, all with
\mathbf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{8}\neq\mathbf{0}
. Finally, there is
1
term
\langle\psi|\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{0}}|\psi\rangle^{8}=1
. Conditioning on the event that
|\langle\psi|\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}|\psi\rangle|\leq\epsilon
for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
, which happens with probability at least
1-2d^{2n}{\exp}\big({-}\frac{d^{n}\epsilon^{2}}{36\pi^{3}}\big)
(
Lemma

59
), the mass of
b_{\psi}
over
\mathscr{X}^{4}
is

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{X}\in\mathscr{X}^{4}}b_{\psi}(\mathbf{X})\leq\frac{1}{p_{j}^{4}}\sum_{i=0}^{4}\binom{4}{i}(p_{j}-1)^{i}\epsilon^{16i}=\frac{(1+\epsilon^{4}(p_{j}-1))^{4}}{p_{j}^{4}}\leq\left(\frac{1}{p_{j}}+\epsilon^{4}\right)^{4}.

The above is valid for any maximal proper submodule
\mathscr{X}\subset\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n}
, which concludes the proof.
∎

5
Stabiliser state learning

Using our unitary
\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{R}}
from
Theorem

43
plus Bell difference sampling (
Theorem

49
), it is simple to learn an unknown stabiliser group
\mathcal{S}
given enough copies of
|\mathcal{S}\rangle
, as shown in
Algorithm

1
.

1

Input:

8\lceil\frac{3n}{4}\rceil+2n+8
copies of a stabiliser state
|\mathcal{S}\rangle\in(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n}
.

Output:

a succinct description of the stabiliser group
\mathcal{S}
.

2

for

i\leftarrow-1

to

\lceil\frac{3n}{4}\rceil-1

do

3

Perform Bell sampling on
(\mathbf{I}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{R}}^{\dagger})|\mathcal{S}\rangle^{\otimes 8}
to obtain
\mathbf{x}_{4i+1},\mathbf{x}_{4i+2},\mathbf{x}_{4i+3},\mathbf{x}_{4i+4}\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n}

4

if

i\neq-1

then

5

\mathbf{x}_{4i+j}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{4i+j}-\mathbf{x}_{-4+j}
for
j=1,2,3,4

6

Determine a generating set
\mathscr{B}
for
\langle\{\mathbf{x}_{i}\}_{i\in[3n]}\rangle
by putting the matrix
[\mathbf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{3n}]\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n\times 3n}
into its Smith normal form

7

For each
\mathbf{x}\in\mathscr{B}
, measure a copy of
|\mathcal{S}\rangle
in the eigenbasis of
\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}
to determine
s(\mathbf{x})\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}
such that
\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}|\mathcal{S}\rangle=\omega^{-s(\mathbf{x})}|\mathcal{S}\rangle

8

return

\mathcal{S}=\langle\{\omega^{s(\mathbf{x})}\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}\}_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathscr{B}}\rangle

Algorithm 1

Learning stabiliser states
|\mathcal{S}\rangle

Theorem 61
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be an unknown stabiliser group with stabiliser state
|\mathcal{S}\rangle\in(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n}
. Let
d=\prod_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}^{k_{i}}
be the prime factorisation of
d
.
Algorithm

1
identifies
\mathcal{S}
using
8n+O(1)
copies of
|\mathcal{S}\rangle
. Its runtime is
O(n^{3})
and its failure probability is at most
\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}^{-n}
.

Proof.

Write
\mathcal{S}=\{\omega^{s(\mathbf{x})}\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}:\mathbf{x}\in\mathscr{M}\}
.
According to
Theorem

49
, Bell sampling on
(\mathbf{I}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{R}}^{\dagger})|\mathcal{S}\rangle^{\otimes 8}
, where
\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{R}}
is the unitary of
Definition

40
, returns
(\mathbf{x}_{4i+1},\mathbf{x}_{4i+2},\mathbf{x}_{4i+3},\mathbf{x}_{4i+4})\in(\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n})^{\otimes 4}
with probability
\prod_{j=1}^{4}p_{\mathcal{S}}(\mathbf{x}_{4i+j}+\mathbf{z}_{j}(\mathcal{S}))
for some
\mathbf{z}_{1}(\mathcal{S}),\dots,\mathbf{z}_{4}(\mathcal{S})\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n}
. Thus, perform Bell sampling
(\mathbf{I}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{R}}^{\dagger})|\mathcal{S}\rangle^{\otimes 8}
a number of
\lceil\frac{3n}{4}\rceil+1
times to obtain
(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{4i+1},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{4i+2},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{4i+3},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{4i+4})_{i=-1}^{\lceil\frac{3n}{4}\rceil-1}
. By removing
(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{-3},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{-2},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{-1},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{0})
from all the other elements, we end up with at least
3n
elements
\mathbf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{3n}\in\mathscr{M}
.
We then compute a generating set
\mathscr{B}
for
\langle\{\mathbf{x}_{i}\}_{i\in[3n]}\rangle
, which can be done by putting the matrix
[\mathbf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{3n}]\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n\times 3n}
into its Smith normal formal, which takes
O(n^{3})
time
[
Sto96
]
. Finally, for each element
\mathbf{x}\in\mathscr{B}
, measure a copy of
|\mathcal{S}\rangle
in the eigenbasis of
\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}
to determine
s(\mathbf{x})\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}
such that
\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}|\mathcal{S}\rangle=\omega^{-s(\mathbf{x})}|\mathcal{S}\rangle
, which requires
O(n^{2})
time.
The algorithm fails if the
3n
samples
\mathbf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{3n}
are contained in a maximal proper submodule of
\mathscr{M}
. By
2
,
\mathscr{M}\cong\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m}\mathscr{M}_{i}
where
\mathscr{M}_{i}
has size
|\mathscr{M}_{i}|=p_{i}^{nk_{i}}
and decomposes as
\mathscr{M}_{i}\cong\bigoplus_{j=1}^{r_{i}}\mathbb{Z}/p_{i}^{\lambda_{ij}}\mathbb{Z}
with
\lambda_{ij}\geq 1
and
\sum_{j=1}^{r_{i}}\lambda_{ij}=nk_{i}
. Note that
r_{i}\leq 2n
for all
i\in[m]
since
\mathscr{M}
has at most
2n
generators.
The number of maximal proper submodules of
\bigoplus_{j=1}^{r_{i}}\mathbb{Z}/p_{i}^{\lambda_{ij}}\mathbb{Z}
is at most the number of maximal proper submodules of
(\mathbb{Z}/p_{i}^{nk_{i}}\mathbb{Z})^{r_{i}}
, which is
\frac{p_{i}^{r_{i}}-1}{p_{i}-1}
(
Lemma

62
). Hence, the probability that all
3n
samples are contained in a proper submodule of
\mathscr{M}
is, by a union bound,

\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{p_{i}^{r_{i}}-1}{p_{i}-1}\left(\frac{p_{i}^{nk_{i}-1}}{p_{i}^{nk_{i}}}\right)^{3n}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}^{-3n}\frac{p_{i}^{r_{i}}-1}{p_{i}-1}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}^{-3n}\frac{p_{i}^{2n}-1}{p_{i}-1}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}^{-n}.\qed

Lemma 62
.

There are
\frac{p^{n}-1}{p-1}
maximal proper submodules in the
\mathbb{Z}_{p^{k}}
-module
\mathbb{Z}_{p^{k}}^{n}
for
p
prime and
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

Let
M\subset\mathbb{Z}_{p^{k}}^{n}
be a maximal proper submodule. First note that
\mathbb{Z}_{p^{k}}^{n}/M\cong\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is simple (it has no nontrivial proper submodule). We claim that there is a one-to-one correspondence between maximal proper submodules
M\subset\mathbb{Z}_{p^{k}}^{n}
and kernels of surjective homomorphisms
\phi:\mathbb{Z}_{p^{k}}^{n}\to\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. Indeed, by isomorphism theorems,
\mathbb{Z}_{p^{k}}^{n}/\operatorname{ker}(\phi)\cong\mathbb{Z}_{d}
, meaning that
\operatorname{ker}(\phi)
must be a maximal proper submodule, while given a maximal proper submodule
M
, the natural projection
\pi:\mathbb{Z}_{p^{k}}^{n}\to\mathbb{Z}_{p^{k}}^{n}/M
defined by
\pi(x)=x+M
for all
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{p^{k}}^{n}
is a surjective homomorphism with
\operatorname{ker}(\pi)=M
. We now count the number of surjective homomorphisms
\phi:\mathbb{Z}_{p^{k}}^{n}\to\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. A homomorphism is determined by the images of all
n
generators of
\mathbb{Z}_{p^{k}}^{n}
, which can be mapped to any element in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, totaling
p^{n}-1
possible surjective homomorphisms, where we excluded the zero homomorphism since it is not surjective. On the other hand, different homomorphisms can have the same kernel. More specifically, two surjective homomorphisms
\phi
and
\varphi
have the same kernel if and only if
\phi=\lambda\varphi
for
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}\setminus\{0\}
. Since there are
p-1
possible values of
\lambda
, there are
\frac{p^{n}-1}{p-1}
possible kernels of surjective homomorphisms
\phi:\mathbb{Z}_{p^{k}}^{n}\to\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, and thus of maximal proper submodules.
∎

6
Hidden Stabiliser Group Problem

The problem of identifying an unknown stabiliser state
|\mathcal{S}\rangle\in(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n}
from the previous section can be framed as an instance of the so-called
State Hidden Subgroup Problem
(StateHSP) introduced by Bouland, Giurgică-Tiron, and Wright
[
BGTW25
]
. Hinsche, Eisert, and Carrasco
[
HEC25
]
narrowed down StateHSP to the case when the hidden group is a partial stabiliser group and defined the
Hidden Stabiliser Group Problem
as follows.
3
3

3

[
HEC25
]
defines the Hidden Stabiliser Group Problem by supposing that
|\psi\rangle\in(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n}
has a non-trivial partial stabiliser group
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathscr{P}_{d}^{n}
such that
\mathcal{P}|\psi\rangle=|\psi\rangle
for all
\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{X}
and
|\langle\psi|\mathcal{P}|\psi\rangle|\leq 1-\varepsilon
for all
\mathcal{P}\in\mathscr{P}_{d}^{n}\setminus\mathcal{X}
. We believe that this is not an appropriate definition since for any
\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{X}
and
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}\setminus\{0\}
,
\omega^{s}\mathcal{P}\notin\mathcal{X}
and still
|\langle\psi|\omega^{s}\mathcal{P}|\psi\rangle|=1
.

Definition 63

(Hidden Stabiliser Group Problem)
.

Given
\varepsilon>0
, let
|\psi\rangle\in(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n}
such that
|\langle\psi|\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}|\psi\rangle|<1-\varepsilon
for all
\mathbf{x}\notin\operatorname{Weyl}(|\psi\rangle)
. The
hidden stabiliser group problem
asks to identify
\operatorname{Weyl}(|\psi\rangle)
given access to copies of
|\psi\rangle
.

Hinsche, Eisert, and Carrasco
[
HEC25
]
proposed a quantum algorithm to solve the Hidden Stabiliser Group Problem that uses
O\big(\frac{nd\log{d}}{1-(1-\varepsilon)^{d}}\big)
copies of
|\psi\rangle
and runs in polynomial time.
4
4

4

We note that the factor
\log{d}
can be improved by using our
Lemma

64
, which is sharper than
[
HEC25
, Lemma 1]
.
Their algorithm uses the POVM given by
\Pi_{\mathbf{x}}^{\rm HEC}=d^{-2n}\sum_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n}}\omega^{[\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}]}\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{y}}^{\otimes D}
to sample elements from, and thus learn,
\operatorname{Weyl}(|\psi\rangle)^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}}
. A similar idea can be employed for all dimensions
d\geq 2
but now using our generalised Bell difference sampling subroutine: by sampling enough elements from the distribution
b_{\psi}
, their span will generate
\operatorname{Weyl}(|\psi\rangle)^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}}
with high probability, from which
\operatorname{Weyl}(|\psi\rangle)
can be obtained. In order to analyse the probability of successfully generating the whole submodule
\operatorname{Weyl}(|\psi\rangle)^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}}
, we shall need the following lemma, which generalises
[
GIKL23a
, Lemma 2.3]
and
[
CGYZ25
, Lemma 4.21]
.

Lemma 64
.

Let
\delta\in(0,1)
,
\varepsilon>0
, and
\mathcal{D}
be a distribution over
\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n}
. Let
d=\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}p_{i}^{k_{i}}
be the prime decomposition of
d
. Let
\mathscr{X}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n}
be the submodule spanned by
m
i.i.d. samples from
\mathcal{D}
. If
m\geq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\big(2\ln\frac{1}{\delta}+4n\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}k_{i}\big)
, then
\mathcal{D}(\mathscr{X})\triangleq\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathscr{X}}\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{x})\geq 1-\varepsilon
.

Proof.

For
0\leq i\leq m
, define
\mathscr{X}_{i}\triangleq\langle\mathbf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{m}\rangle
, with
\mathscr{X}_{0}\triangleq\{0^{2n}\}
. Define the indicator random variable
Z_{i}
as

\displaystyle Z_{i}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if}~\mathbf{x}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n}\setminus\mathscr{X}_{i-1}~\text{or}~\mathcal{D}(\mathscr{X}_{i-1})\geq 1-\varepsilon,\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

The value
Z_{i}=1
indicates that either the new sample
\mathbf{x}_{i}
has increased the span of
\mathscr{X}_{i}
or that
\mathscr{X}_{i-1}
already accounts for a
(1-\varepsilon)
-fraction of the mass of
\mathcal{D}
. The following facts are true:

1.

For any
\mathbf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{i-1}
,
\operatorname{Pr}[Z_{i}=1|\mathbf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{i-1}]\geq\varepsilon
. Indeed, if
\mathcal{D}(\mathscr{X}_{i-1})>1-\varepsilon
, then already
Z_{i}=1
, while if
\mathcal{D}(\mathscr{X}_{i-1})\leq 1-\varepsilon
, then the probability that
Z_{i}=1
is
1-\mathcal{D}(\mathscr{X}_{i-1})\geq\varepsilon
.

2.

As a consequence of the above fact,
\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}]\geq\varepsilon
.

3.

Whenever
\sum_{i=1}^{m}Z_{i}\geq 2n\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}k_{i}
, we have
\mathcal{D}(\mathscr{X}_{m})\geq 1-\varepsilon
. To see that, assume by contradiction that
\mathcal{D}(\mathscr{X}_{m})<1-\varepsilon
, in which case
\mathcal{D}(\mathscr{X}_{i})<1-\varepsilon
for all
i\leq m
, and so
Z_{i}=1
implies that
\mathbf{x}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n}\setminus\mathscr{X}_{i-1}
. There are at least
2n\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}k_{i}
such
i
, which is the length of the module
\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n}
, therefore
\mathbf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{m}
must span the whole space
\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n}
, which contradicts the assumption
\mathcal{D}(\mathscr{X}_{m})<1-\varepsilon
.

Building on the above facts, we now prove the statement via a Chernoff bound, the only caveat being the fact that the
Z_{i}
’s not independent. In order to deal with that, consider the new random variables
Z_{i}^{\prime}
(for
i\in[m]
) as
m
i.i.d. samples from the Bernoulli distribution
\operatorname{Pr}[Z_{i}^{\prime}=1]=\varepsilon
. Then
\operatorname{Pr}[Z_{i}^{\prime}=1|Z_{1}^{\prime},\dots,Z_{i-1}^{\prime}]=\varepsilon\leq\operatorname{Pr}[Z_{i}=1|Z_{1},\dots,Z_{i-1}]
from the first fact, and thus
\operatorname{Pr}[\sum_{i=1}^{m}<2n\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}k_{i}]\leq\operatorname{Pr}[\sum_{i=1}^{m}Z_{i}^{\prime}<2n\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}k_{i}]
. Let
\theta\triangleq 1-\frac{2n}{m\varepsilon}\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}k_{i}
. Then

\displaystyle\operatorname{Pr}[\mathcal{D}(\mathscr{X}_{m})\geq 1-\varepsilon]

\displaystyle\geq\operatorname{Pr}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{m}Z_{i}\geq 2n\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}k_{i}\right]

(by third fact)

\displaystyle\geq 1-\operatorname{Pr}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{m}Z_{i}^{\prime}<2n\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}k_{i}\right]

(by first fact)

\displaystyle=1-\operatorname{Pr}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{m}Z_{i}^{\prime}<(1-\theta)m\varepsilon\right]

\displaystyle\geq 1-\exp\left(-\frac{\theta^{2}m\varepsilon}{2}\right)

(Chernoff bound)

\displaystyle\geq 1-\exp\left(-\frac{m\varepsilon}{2}+2n\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}k_{i}\right)\geq 1-\delta.\qed

1

Input:

8m+8
copies of
|\psi\rangle\in(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n}
where
m\triangleq\big\lceil\frac{2\ln\frac{1}{\delta}+16n\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}k_{i}}{1-(1-\varepsilon(2-\varepsilon)(1-1/p_{1}))^{4}}\big\rceil
and
d=\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}p_{i}^{k_{i}}
is the prime decomposition of
d
and
p_{1}
its smaller prime factor.

Promise:

|\langle\psi|\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}|\psi\rangle|<1-\varepsilon
for all
\mathbf{x}\notin\operatorname{Weyl}(|\psi\rangle)
.

Output:

a succinct description of
\operatorname{Weyl}(|\psi\rangle)
.

2

for

i\leftarrow-1

to

m-1

do

3

Perform Bell sampling on
(\mathbf{I}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{R}}^{\dagger})|\mathcal{S}\rangle^{\otimes 8}
to obtain
\mathbf{x}_{4i+1},\mathbf{x}_{4i+2},\mathbf{x}_{4i+3},\mathbf{x}_{4i+4}\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n}

4

if

i\neq-1

then

5

\mathbf{x}_{4i+j}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{4i+j}-\mathbf{x}_{-4+j}
for
j=1,2,3,4

6

Determine
\mathscr{W}=\langle\{\mathbf{x}_{i}\}_{i\in[4m]}\rangle^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}}
by putting the matrix
[\mathbf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{4m}]\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n\times 4m}
into its Smith normal form

7

return

\mathscr{W}

Algorithm 2

Hidden Stabiliser Group Problem

Theorem 65
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
and
\delta\in(0,1)
. Let
|\psi\rangle\in(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n}
be an unknown state such that
|\langle\psi|\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}|\psi\rangle|<1-\varepsilon
for all
\mathbf{x}\notin\operatorname{Weyl}(|\psi\rangle)
. Let
d=\prod_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}^{k_{i}}
be the prime factorisation of
d
.
Algorithm

2
identifies
\operatorname{Weyl}(|\psi\rangle)
with probability at least
1-\delta
in
O\big(\frac{n^{2}}{\varepsilon}\big(\!\log\frac{1}{\delta}+n\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}k_{i}\big)\big)
time using
O\big(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\big(\!\log\frac{1}{\delta}+n\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}k_{i}\big)\big)
copies of
|\psi\rangle
and acting on at most
8
copies of
|\psi\rangle
at a time.

Proof.

For the sake of simplifying our analysis, we consider a slightly weaker algorithm wherein one samples
\mathbf{X}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{X}^{(m)}\sim b_{\psi}
, computes the four separate spans
\mathscr{W}_{j}\triangleq\langle\{\mathbf{X}_{j}^{(i)}\}_{i\in[m]}\rangle
for
j\in[4]
, and considers the largest one. In practice there is no need to separate the four samples coming from one Bell difference sampling, but since
Lemma

64
requires i.i.d. samples and we cannot guarantee the independence of
\mathbf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{4}
coming from one Bell difference sampling, we opted to separate the samples. We know that all
\mathbf{X}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{X}^{(m)}\sim b_{\psi}
belong to
(\operatorname{Weyl}(|\psi\rangle)^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}})^{\otimes 4}
. For any proper submodule
\mathscr{X}^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}}\subset\operatorname{Weyl}(|\psi\rangle)^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}}
(so that
\operatorname{Weyl}(|\psi\rangle)\subset\mathscr{X}
), we have that (notice that we do not need to consider proper submodules of
(\operatorname{Weyl}(|\psi\rangle)^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}})^{\otimes 4}
but only of
\operatorname{Weyl}(|\psi\rangle)^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}}
since we pick the largest span out of
\mathscr{W}_{1},\mathscr{W}_{2},\mathscr{W}_{3},\mathscr{W}_{4}
)

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{X}\in(\mathscr{X}^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}})^{\otimes 4}}b_{\psi}(\mathbf{X})

\displaystyle=|\mathscr{X}^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}}|^{4}\sum_{\mathbf{X}\in\mathscr{X}^{\otimes 4}}\prod_{i=1}^{4}p_{\psi}(\mathbf{X}_{i})p_{\psi}((\mathbf{X}\mathbf{R})_{i})

(by
Corollary

51
)

\displaystyle\leq|\mathscr{X}^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}}|^{4}\left(\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathscr{X}}p_{\psi}(\mathbf{x})^{2}\right)^{4}

(Cauchy-Schwarz)

\displaystyle=|\mathscr{X}^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}}|^{4}\left(\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\operatorname{Weyl}(|\psi\rangle)}p_{\psi}(\mathbf{x})^{2}+\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathscr{X}\setminus\operatorname{Weyl}(|\psi\rangle)}p_{\psi}(\mathbf{x})^{2}\right)^{4}

\displaystyle<|\mathscr{X}^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}}|^{4}\left(d^{-2n}|\operatorname{Weyl}(|\psi\rangle)|+d^{-2n}(1-\varepsilon)^{2}(|\mathscr{X}|-|\operatorname{Weyl}(|\psi\rangle)|)\right)^{4}

\displaystyle<\left(1-\varepsilon(2-\varepsilon)\left(1-\frac{1}{p_{1}}\right)\right)^{4}.

(
|\operatorname{Weyl}(|\psi\rangle)|\leq\frac{|\mathscr{X}|}{p_{1}}
by Lagrange’s theorem)

Let
\varepsilon^{\prime}\triangleq 1-\big(1-\varepsilon(2-\varepsilon)(1-\frac{1}{p_{1}})\big)^{4}\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
. By collecting
m\geq\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{\prime}}\big(2\ln\frac{1}{\delta}+16n\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}k_{i}\big)
samples
\mathbf{X}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{X}^{(m)}
(
b_{\psi}
is over
(\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n})^{\otimes 4}
), then according to
Lemma

64
the
b_{\psi}
-mass of
\langle\mathbf{X}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{X}^{(m)}\rangle
is at least
1-\varepsilon^{\prime}
, therefore the largest span out of
\mathscr{W}_{1},\mathscr{W}_{2},\mathscr{W}_{3},\mathscr{W}_{4}
must equal
\operatorname{Weyl}(|\psi\rangle)^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}}
.

The number of copies of
|\psi\rangle
employed by
Algorithm

2
is
8m+8=O\big(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\big(\!\log\frac{1}{\delta}+n\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}k_{i}\big)\big)
(
8
copies for the initial sample
\mathbf{X}^{(0)}=(\mathbf{x}_{-3},\mathbf{x}_{-2},\mathbf{x}_{-1},\mathbf{x}_{0})
to be subtracted from all
m
subsequent samples), while the total runtime is dominated by computing the Smith normal form of
\mathscr{W}
in
O(n^{2}m)=O\big(\frac{n^{2}}{\varepsilon}\big(\!\log\frac{1}{\delta}+n\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}k_{i}\big)\big)
time.
∎

The sample complexity of
Algorithm

2
is basically
O\big(\frac{n}{\varepsilon}\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}k_{i}\big)
, or simply
O\big(\frac{n}{\varepsilon}\big)
for prime dimensions. The quantum algorithm of
[
HEC25
]
, on the other hand, has sample complexity
O\big(\frac{nd\log{d}}{1-(1-\varepsilon)^{d}}\big)=O(n\log{d}\max\{d,\varepsilon^{-1}\})
(since it is
O\big(\frac{n\log{d}}{\varepsilon}\big)
for
\varepsilon=O(d^{-1})
and
O(nd\log{d})
for
\varepsilon=\Omega(d^{-1})
). Our quantum algorithm has thus a better sample (and time) complexity and acts on at most
8
copies of
|\psi\rangle
at a time (
4
copies for prime dimensions and
2
if further
d\equiv 1~(\operatorname{mod}4)
), while
\{\Pi_{\mathbf{x}}^{\rm HEC}\}_{\mathbf{x}}
requires
D
copies of
|\psi\rangle
at a time, which can be impractical for large
d
.

7
Property testing stabiliser size

In this section, we focus on testing whether a given state
|\psi\rangle
has stabiliser size at least
d^{t}
or is
\varepsilon
-far from any state with stabiliser size at least
d^{t}
, promised that one of these cases holds. As we shall see, this section serves as a warm-up to
Section

8
. The results presented here generalise
[
GIKL23a
]
to qudits, who proposed a property testing algorithm for stabiliser dimension. Since the concept of dimension is not well defined for general
d
, the right concept to focus on is that of stabiliser size.

The idea of
Algorithm

3
is quite simple: sample several values from the skewed Bell distribution
b_{\psi}
and compute the size of their span. If it is at most
d^{2n-t}
, then we output the case where
|\psi\rangle
has stabiliser size at least
d^{t}
. If, on the other hand, the span has size greater than
d^{2n-t}
, then we output the case where
|\psi\rangle
is
\varepsilon
-far from any state with stabiliser size at least
d^{t}
. Implicit in the algorithm is learning the submodule
\operatorname{Weyl}(|\psi\rangle)
, which was the task behind the Hidden Stabiliser Group Problem from
Section

6
. As a consequence,
Algorithms

3
and
2
are quite similar. The correctness of
Algorithm

3
is proven in the next theorem.

1

Input:

8m+8
copies of
|\psi\rangle\in(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n}
, where
m\triangleq\big\lceil\frac{2}{3\varepsilon}\big({\ln}\frac{1}{\delta}+8n\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}k_{i}\big)\big\rceil
.

Promise:

|\psi\rangle
has stabiliser size at least
d^{t}
or is
\varepsilon
-far in fidelity from all such states.

Output:

1
if
|\psi\rangle
has stabiliser size at least
d^{t}
and
0
otherwise, with probability
\geq 1-\delta
.

2

for

i\leftarrow-1

to

m-1

do

3

Perform Bell sampling on
(\mathbf{I}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{R}}^{\dagger})|\psi\rangle^{\otimes 8}
to obtain
\mathbf{x}_{4i+1},\mathbf{x}_{4i+2},\mathbf{x}_{4i+3},\mathbf{x}_{4i+4}\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n}

4

if

i\neq-1

then

5

\mathbf{x}_{4i+j}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{4i+j}-\mathbf{x}_{-4+j}
for
j=1,2,3,4

6

Compute the size
K
of
\langle\{\mathbf{x}_{i}\}_{i\in[4m]}\rangle
by putting the matrix
[\mathbf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{4m}]\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n\times 4m}
into its Smith normal form

7

return

1
if
K\leq d^{2n-t}
and
0
otherwise

Algorithm 3

Property testing stabiliser size

Theorem 66
.

Let
d=\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}p_{i}^{k_{i}}
be the prime decomposition of
d
and
p_{1}
the smallest prime to divide
d
. Let
\delta\in(0,1)
and
0<\varepsilon\leq\frac{1}{6d^{2}}\big(1-\frac{1}{p_{1}}\big)\big(1-\frac{1}{8d^{2}}\big)
. Let
|\psi\rangle\in(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n}
be a state promised to either have stabiliser size at least
d^{t}
or be
\varepsilon
-far in fidelity from all such states.
Algorithm

3
distinguishes the two cases with probability at least
1-\delta
, uses
8\big\lceil\frac{2}{3\varepsilon}\big({\ln}\frac{1}{\delta}+8n\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}k_{i}\big)\big\rceil+8
copies of
|\psi\rangle
, and has runtime
O\big(\frac{n^{2}}{\varepsilon}\big(\!\log\frac{1}{\delta}+n\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}k_{i}\big)\big)
.

Proof.

Assume first that
|\psi\rangle
has stabiliser size at least
d^{t}
. According to
Lemma

54
, the distribution
b_{\psi}
is supported on
(\operatorname{Weyl}(|\psi\rangle)^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}})^{\otimes 4}
. Therefore,
\mathscr{X}^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}}\triangleq\langle\{\mathbf{x}_{i}\}_{i\in[4m]}\rangle
is supported on
\operatorname{Weyl}(|\psi\rangle)^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}}
, which has size at most
d^{2n-t}
, and so
Algorithm

3
accepts with probability
1
.

Now assume that
|\psi\rangle
is
\varepsilon
-far in fidelity from all states with stabiliser size at least
d^{t}
. Since
m\geq\frac{1}{3\varepsilon}\big(2\ln\frac{1}{\delta}+16n\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}k_{i}\big)
(recall that
b_{\psi}
is over
(\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n})^{\otimes 4}
),
Lemmas

64
and
53
imply that, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathscr{X}^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}}}p_{\psi}(\mathbf{x})\geq\left(\sum_{\mathbf{X}\in(\mathscr{X}^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}})^{\otimes 4}}b_{\psi}(\mathbf{X})\right)^{\frac{1}{4}}\geq(1-3\varepsilon)^{\frac{1}{4}}\geq 1-\varepsilon,

where we used that
\varepsilon<\frac{1}{2}
in the last step. Under this event,
Lemma

16
leads to

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathscr{X}}p_{\psi}(\mathbf{x})=\frac{|\mathscr{X}|}{d^{n}}\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathscr{X}^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}}}p_{\psi}(\mathbf{x})\geq\frac{1-\varepsilon}{K}.

(5)

On the other hand, since

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{X}\in(\mathscr{X}^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}})^{\otimes 4}}b_{\psi}(\mathbf{X})\geq 1-3\varepsilon\geq 1-\frac{1}{2d^{2}}\left(1-\frac{1}{p_{1}}\right)\left(1-\frac{1}{8d^{2}}\right),

Lemma

56
implies that
\mathscr{X}
is isotropic, which together with
Equation

5
allows us to use
Lemma

34
to argue that there is a state with stabiliser size at least
d^{2n}/K
and fidelity at least
1-\varepsilon
with
|\psi\rangle
. By assumption,
|\psi\rangle
is
\varepsilon
-far from states with stabiliser size at least
d^{t}
, therefore
d^{2n}/K<d^{t}\implies K>d^{2n-t}
and thus
Algorithm

3
rejects with probability at least
1-\delta
.

Regarding the complexities,
Algorithm

3
uses
8m+8
copies of
|\psi\rangle
and requires
O(mn^{2})
time to compute the Smith normal form of
\mathscr{X}^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}}
.
∎

8
Pseudorandomness lower bounds

In this section, we prove that the output of any Clifford circuit augmented with a few non-Clifford single-qudit gates can be efficiently distinguished from a Haar-random quantum state, and therefore Clifford circuits require several non-Clifford single-qudit gates in order to generate pseudorandom quantum states (
Definition

71
). These results generalise
[
GIKL23c
,
GIKL23b
]
from qubits to qudits. Moreover, as we shall see, our final lower bound is exponentially better compared to the prior work of
[
ADIS24
]
, who also focused on qudits. The ideas behind our proof are similar to the ones from
[
GIKL23b
]
: a Haar-random state has vanishing stabiliser size with very high probability, while the output of a Clifford circuit with just a few non-Clifford single-qudit gates has a high stabiliser size.

8.1
Stabiliser size of
t
-doped Clifford circuits

Following
[
LOH23
,
OLLH24
,
LOLH24
]
, we first define
t
-doped Clifford circuits over qudits. Recall that a Clifford gate is any element belonging to the Clifford group
\mathscr{C}^{n}_{d}
.

Definition 67

(
t
-doped Clifford circuits)
.

A
t
-doped Clifford circuit is a quantum circuit composed of Clifford gates and at most
t
non-Clifford single-qudit gates that starts in the state
|0\rangle^{\otimes n}
.

The main technical lemma from this section is the fact that every non-Clifford gate in a Clifford circuit decreases the stabiliser length by at most
1
.

Lemma 68
.

Let
|\psi\rangle\in(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n}
be the output state of a
t
-doped Clifford circuit. Then the stabiliser size of
|\psi\rangle
is at least
d^{n-2t}
, i.e.,
|\operatorname{Weyl}(|\psi\rangle)|\geq d^{n-2t}
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on
t
. For
t=0
, the output state is a stabiliser state, which has stabiliser size
d^{n}
. Assume then the induction hypothesis for
t-1
. Write
|\psi\rangle=\mathcal{C}\mathcal{U}|\phi\rangle
, where
|\phi\rangle
is the output of a
(t-1)
-doped Clifford circuit,
\mathcal{U}
is a single-qudit gate, and
\mathcal{C}
is a Clifford circuit. Since the stabiliser size is unchanged by Clifford circuits (
Corollary

29
), we just need to show that the stabiliser size of
\mathcal{U}|\phi\rangle
is at least
d^{n-2t}
. For such, consider
\operatorname{Weyl}(|\phi\rangle)
with
|\operatorname{Weyl}(|\phi\rangle)|\geq d^{n-2(t-1)}
by the induction assumption. Note that, for any
\mathbf{x}\in\operatorname{Weyl}(|\phi\rangle)
, if
\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}
commutes with
\mathcal{U}
, then

\displaystyle\langle\phi|\mathcal{U}^{\dagger}\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}\mathcal{U}|\phi\rangle=\langle\phi|\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}|\phi\rangle\in\{1,\omega,\dots,\omega^{d-1}\}.

Consider the set of commuting elements with
\mathcal{U}
,
\mathscr{E}\triangleq\{\mathbf{x}\in\operatorname{Weyl}(|\phi\rangle):\mathcal{U}^{\dagger}\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}\}
. Then the stabiliser size of
\mathcal{U}|\phi\rangle
is at least the size of
\mathscr{E}
, but
|\mathscr{E}|\geq|\operatorname{Weyl}(|\phi\rangle)|/d^{2}
, because
\mathscr{E}
contains all elements
\mathbf{x}\in\operatorname{Weyl}(|\phi\rangle)
for which
\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}
restricts to the identity on the qudit to which
\mathcal{U}
applies, i.e.,
x_{i}=(v_{i},w_{i})=(0,0)\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2}
if
\mathcal{U}
acts on the
i
-th qudit. Therefore, the stabiliser size of
\mathcal{U}|\phi\rangle
is at least
d^{n-2t}
.
∎

In
Lemma

54
we showed that the skewed Bell distribution
b_{\psi}
is supported on
(\operatorname{Weyl}(|\psi\rangle)^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}})^{4}
. Together with the above lemma, we conclude that if
|\psi\rangle
is the output of a
t
-doped Clifford circuit, then
b_{\psi}
is supported on a submodule of size at most
d^{4n+8t}
.

Corollary 69
.

If
|\psi\rangle\in(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n}
is the output of a
t
-doped Clifford circuit, then the support of the skewed Bell distribution
b_{\psi}
is a cartesian product of four submodules, each of size at most
d^{n+2t}
.

Proof.

According to
Lemma

54
, the support of
b_{\psi}
is contained in
(\operatorname{Weyl}(|\psi\rangle)^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}})^{4}
, each of which with size
d^{2n}/|\operatorname{Weyl}(|\psi\rangle)|\leq d^{n+2t}
, where the last inequality follows from
Lemma

68
.
∎

8.2
Distinguishing
t
-doped Clifford circuits from Haar randomness

In hold of all the results from the previous sections, we now prove that, given enough copies of
|\psi\rangle\in(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n}
, it is possible to distinguish whether (i)
|\psi\rangle
is a Haar random state (ii) or
|\psi\rangle
is the output of a
(n/2-1)
-doped Clifford circuit, under the promise that one case is the correct one. The idea of the algorithm is very simple: after performing skewed Bell difference sampling a number of
O(n)
times, we check whether their span generates the whole space
\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n}
or not. If
|\psi\rangle
is Haar-random, then the sampled elements generate
\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n}
with high probability, which can never happen if
|\psi\rangle
is the output of a
t
-doped Clifford circuit for
t<n/2
, since
b_{\psi}
is supported on a submodule of size at most
d^{4(n+2t)}
.

1

Input:

8m+1
copies of
|\psi\rangle\in(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n}
, where
m\triangleq\lceil\frac{3n}{4}\rceil
.

Promise:

|\psi\rangle
is Haar-random or the output of a
(n/2-1)
-doped Clifford circuit

Output:

0
if
|\psi\rangle
is Haar-random and
1
otherwise, with probability at least
1-\delta
.

2

for

i\leftarrow-1

to

m-1

do

3

Perform Bell sampling on
(\mathbf{I}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{R}}^{\dagger})|\psi\rangle^{\otimes 8}
to obtain
\mathbf{x}_{4i+1},\mathbf{x}_{4i+2},\mathbf{x}_{4i+3},\mathbf{x}_{4i+4}\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n}

4

if

i\neq-1

then

5

\mathbf{x}_{4i+j}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{4i+j}-\mathbf{x}_{-4+j}
for
j=1,2,3,4

6

Compute the size
K
of
\langle\{\mathbf{x}_{i}\}_{i\in[4m]}\rangle
by putting the matrix
[\mathbf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{4m}]\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n\times 4m}
into its Smith normal form

7

return

0
if
K=d^{2n}
and
1
otherwise

Algorithm 4

Distinguishing
(n/2-1)
-doped Clifford circuits from Haar-random

Theorem 70
.

Let
|\psi\rangle\in(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n}
be a state promised to be either Haar-random or the output of a
t
-doped Clifford circuit for
t<n/2
. Let
d=\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}p_{i}^{k_{i}}
be the prime decomposition of
d
. Then
Algorithm

4
distinguishes the two cases with failure probability at most
2\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\big(\frac{3}{2p_{i}}\big)^{n}
for sufficiently large
n
, uses
6n+O(1)
copies of
|\psi\rangle
, and has runtime
O(n^{3})
.

Proof.

If
|\psi\rangle
is the output of a
t
-doped Clifford circuit with
t<n/2
, then
\langle\{\mathbf{x}_{i}\}_{i\in[4m]}\rangle
always has size less than
d^{2n}
according to
Corollary

45
, where
\mathbf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{4m}\in\operatorname{Weyl}(|\psi\rangle)^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}}
are skewed Bell difference samples. This means that
Algorithm

4
can only fail when
|\psi\rangle
is Haar random, which happens if
\mathbf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{4m}\in\operatorname{Weyl}(|\psi\rangle)^{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}}
all fall in the same maximal proper submodule
\mathscr{X}\subset\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n}
. Assume that
\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n}/\mathscr{X}\cong\mathbb{Z}/p_{i}\mathbb{Z}
for some prime
p_{i}|d
. Order
p_{1}<p_{2}<\cdots<p_{\ell}
. The probability that all samples
\mathbf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{4m}
are in
\mathscr{X}
is
\big(\sum_{\mathbf{X}\in\mathscr{X}^{4}}b_{\psi}(\mathbf{X})\big)^{m}
. With probability at least
1-2d^{2n}{\exp}\big({-}\frac{d^{n}\epsilon^{2}}{36\pi^{3}}\big)
, we have that
\big(\sum_{\mathbf{X}\in\mathscr{X}^{4}}b_{\psi}(\mathbf{X})\big)^{m}\leq\big(\frac{1}{p_{i}}+\epsilon^{4}\big)^{4m}
. Take
\epsilon^{4}=\big(\big(\frac{3}{2}\big)^{\frac{1}{3}}-1\big)\frac{1}{p_{\ell}}
so that
\frac{1}{p_{i}}+\epsilon^{4}\leq\big(\frac{3}{2}\big)^{\frac{1}{3}}\frac{1}{p_{i}}
. There are
\frac{p_{i}^{2n}-1}{p_{i}-1}
submodules such that
\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n}/\mathscr{X}\cong\mathbb{Z}/p_{i}\mathbb{Z}
. A union bound over all such submodules and
i\in[\ell]
leads to

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\frac{p_{i}^{2n}-1}{p_{i}-1}\left(\frac{1}{p_{i}}+\epsilon^{4}\right)^{4m}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\frac{p_{i}^{2n}-1}{p_{i}-1}\left(\frac{1}{p_{i}}+\epsilon^{4}\right)^{3n}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\frac{p_{i}^{2n}-1}{p_{i}-1}\left(\frac{3}{2p_{i}^{3}}\right)^{n}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\left(\frac{3}{2p_{i}}\right)^{n},

using that
m=\lceil\frac{3n}{4}\rceil
. The total failure probability also needs to take into account the Haar measure failure probability
2d^{2n}{\exp}\big({-}\frac{d^{n}\epsilon^{2}}{36\pi^{3}}\big)
, which is less than
\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\big(\frac{3}{2p_{i}}\big)^{n}
for sufficiently large
n
.

Regarding the complexities, we note that there is no need to perform
m
times skewed Bell difference sampling with different copies of
|\psi\rangle
, which would require
16m
copies. Instead, we can perform skewed Bell sampling
m+1
times and subtract one tuple of outcomes from all the other
m
tuples, reducing the number of copies to
8(m+1)
. Finally, the time complexity to put the matrix
[\mathbf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{4m}]\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n\times 4m}
into its Smith normal form is
O(mn^{2})=O(n^{3})
.
∎

The above results generalises
[
GIKL23b
, Lemma 4.8 & Theorem 4.9]
to qudits and also exponentially improves the range of
t
compared to
[
ADIS24
]
, who provided a
\operatorname{poly}(n)
-sample-and-time-complexity algorithm for distinguishing Haar random states in
(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n}
from
t
-doped Clifford circuits with
t=O(\log{n}/\log{d})
.

As an immediate corollary of the above result, no
t
-doped Clifford circuit with
t<n/2
can produce pseudorandom quantum states, otherwise they would be distinguishable from Haar random states in
\operatorname{poly}(n)
time.

Definition 71

(
[
JLS18
, Definition 2]
)
.

Given a security parameter
\kappa
, a keyed family of
n
-qudit quantum states
\{|\phi_{k}\rangle\in(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n}\}_{k\in\{0,1\}^{\kappa}}
is pseudorandom if (i) there is a polynomial-time quantum algorithm that generates
|\phi_{k}\rangle
on input
k
, and (ii) for any
\operatorname{poly}(\kappa)
-time quantum adversary
\mathcal{A}
,

\displaystyle\left|\operatorname*{\mathbb{P}}_{k\sim\{0,1\}^{\kappa}}[\mathcal{A}(|\phi_{k}\rangle^{\otimes\operatorname{poly}(\kappa)})=1]-\operatorname*{\mathbb{P}}_{|\psi\rangle\sim\mu_{\rm Haar}}[\mathcal{A}(|\psi\rangle^{\otimes\operatorname{poly}(\kappa)})=1]\right|=\operatorname{negl}(\kappa),

where
\mu_{\rm Haar}
is the
n
-qudit Haar measure and
\operatorname{negl}(\kappa)
is an arbitrary negligible function of
\kappa
, i.e.,
\operatorname{negl}(\kappa)=o(\kappa^{-c})

\forall c>0
.

Corollary 72
.

Any doped Clifford circuit that uses at most
n/2-1
non-Clifford single-qudit gates cannot produce an ensemble of pseudorandom quantum states in
(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n}
.

9
Stabiliser state testing

Gross, Nezami, and Walter
[
GNW21
]
developed a stabiliser testing algorithm for qudits (for all
d\geq 2
) which distinguishes between a stabiliser state and a state with stabiliser fidelity at most
1-\varepsilon
using
O(1/\varepsilon)
copies, which we review. For qubits, their algorithm is quite simple: perform Bell difference sampling to obtain
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2n}
, measure the Weyl operator
\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}
twice, and check whether the outcomes are equal or not. Their algorithm for higher dimensions, however, does not have a simple interpretation. The authors introduced the operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{r}\triangleq\frac{1}{d^{n}}\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n}}(\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\dagger})^{\otimes r}\qquad\text{for}~r\in\mathbb{N}

and showed that it possesses all the required properties for stabiliser testing. We are interested in exploring such operator in more details, so we review some of its properties.

Lemma 73

(
[
GNW21
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{V}_{r}\triangleq d^{-n}\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n}}(\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\dagger})^{\otimes r}
. For
r\geq 2
such that
\operatorname{gcd}(d,r)=1
,
\mathcal{V}_{r}
is a Hermitian unitary. As a consequence, the operator
\Pi_{r}^{+}=\frac{1}{2}(\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{V}_{r})
is a projector and the binary POVM
\{\Pi_{r}^{+},\mathbf{I}-\Pi_{r}^{+}\}
can be implemented in
O(nr)
time.

Proof.

It is easy to see that the operator
\mathcal{V}_{r}
is Hermitian by a change of index. Moreover,
\mathcal{V}_{r}
is unitary, since

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{r}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d^{2n}}\sum_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n}}(\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{y}}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\dagger}\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{y}}^{\dagger})^{\otimes r}=\frac{1}{d^{2n}}\sum_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n}}\omega^{r[\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}]}(\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{y}}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{y}}^{\dagger})^{\otimes r}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d^{2n}}\sum_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n}}\omega^{r[\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}]}(\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{z}}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{z}}^{\dagger})^{\otimes r}=\frac{1}{d^{2n}}\sum_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n}}\omega^{[\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}]}(\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{z}}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{z}}^{\dagger})^{\otimes r}=\mathbf{I},

where we used that
\operatorname{gcd}(d,r)=1
to remove
r
from
\omega^{r[\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}]}
.
Therefore,
\Pi_{r}^{+}=\frac{1}{2}(\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{V}_{r})
is a projector, since
(\Pi_{r}^{+})^{2}=\frac{1}{4}(\mathbf{I}+2\mathcal{V}_{r}+\mathcal{V}_{r}^{2})=\Pi_{r}^{+}
.

It is possible to implement the binary POVM
\{\Pi_{r}^{+},\mathbf{I}-\Pi_{r}^{+}\}
by employing quantum phase estimation with
O(1)
ancillae,
O(1)
controlled versions of
\mathcal{V}_{r}
, and
O(1)
auxiliary operations (we do not require a high accuracy in determining the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{V}_{r}
since they are simply
\pm 1
). And since we can write
\mathcal{V}_{r}=\big(d^{-1}\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2}}(\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\dagger})^{\otimes r}\big)^{\otimes n}
, then a controlled version of
\mathcal{V}_{r}
is equal to a composition of
n
controlled versions of
d^{-1}\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2}}(\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\dagger})^{\otimes r}
, each of which acts on
2r
qudits. Therefore, the POVM
\{\Pi_{r}^{+},\mathbf{I}-\Pi_{r}^{+}\}
can be implemented in time
O(nr)
.
∎

Define the binary POVM
\{\Pi_{r}^{+},\mathbf{I}-\Pi_{r}^{+}\}
and let
G_{r}(|\psi\rangle)
be the expected value of the random variable
Z
which equals
1
if we measure
\Pi_{r}^{+}
and
-1
if we measure
\mathbf{I}-\Pi_{r}^{+}
, i.e.,
\mathbb{P}[Z=1]=\operatorname{Tr}[\psi^{\otimes 2r}\Pi_{r}^{+}]
and
\mathbb{P}[Z=-1]=1-\operatorname{Tr}[\psi^{\otimes 2r}\Pi_{r}^{+}]
. Then

\displaystyle G_{r}(|\psi\rangle)\triangleq 2\operatorname{Tr}[\psi^{\otimes 2r}\Pi_{r}^{+}]-1=\operatorname{Tr}[\psi^{\otimes 2r}\mathcal{V}_{r}]=\frac{1}{d^{n}}\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n}}|{\operatorname{Tr}}[\psi\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{x}}]|^{2r}=d^{(r-1)n}\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n}}p_{\psi}(\mathbf{x})^{r}.

(6)

It is possible to relate
G_{r}(|\psi\rangle)
with the stabiliser fidelity
F_{\mathcal{S}}(|\psi\rangle)
using
55
.

Lemma 74
.

Let
r\in\mathbb{N}
,
r\geq 2
, such that
\operatorname{gcd}(d,r)=1
and
C_{d,r}\triangleq\frac{1}{2}\big(1-(1-\frac{1}{4d^{2}})^{r-1}\big)
. Then

\displaystyle F_{\mathcal{S}}(|\psi\rangle)\geq 1+\frac{G_{r}(|\psi\rangle)-1}{2C_{d,r}}\iff G_{r}(|\psi\rangle)\leq 1-2C_{d,r}(1-F_{\mathcal{S}}(|\psi\rangle))\qquad\forall|\psi\rangle\in(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n}.

Proof.

Consider the set
\mathscr{X}_{0}=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n}:d^{n}p_{\psi}(\mathbf{x})\geq 1-\frac{1}{4d^{2}}\}
, which according to
55
is isotropic. It can thus be extended a Lagrangian (maximal) submodule
\mathscr{X}
. Then

\displaystyle F_{\mathcal{S}}(|\psi\rangle)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathscr{X}_{0}}p_{\psi}(\mathbf{x})

(by
Corollary

33
)

\displaystyle=1-\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n}}p_{\psi}(\mathbf{x})\mathbf{1}\left[d^{n}p_{\psi}(\mathbf{x})<1-\frac{1}{4d^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\geq 1-\frac{\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n}}p_{\psi}(\mathbf{x})(1-d^{(r-1)n}p_{\psi}(\mathbf{x})^{r-1})}{1-(1-\frac{1}{4d^{2}})^{r-1}}

(
(r-1)
-th moment Markov’s inequality)

\displaystyle=1+\frac{G_{r}(|\psi\rangle)-1}{1-(1-\frac{1}{4d^{2}})^{r-1}}.

∎

Using the above theorem, we review the stabiliser testing algorithm from
[
GNW21
]
: measure the POVM
\{\Pi_{r}^{+},\mathbf{I}-\Pi_{r}^{+}\}
several times and estimate the quantity
G_{r}(|\psi\rangle)
. If
G_{r}(|\psi\rangle)<1
, then
|\psi\rangle
is far from a stabiliser state.

1

Input:

2rm
copies of
|\psi\rangle\in(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n}
where
m\triangleq\lceil\frac{1}{C_{d,r}\varepsilon}\ln\frac{1}{\delta}\rceil
,
C_{d,r}\triangleq\frac{1}{2}\big(1-(1-\frac{1}{4d^{2}})^{r-1}\big)
, and
r\geq 2
is an integer such that
\operatorname{gcd}(d,r)=1
.

Promise:

either
F_{\mathcal{S}}(|\psi\rangle)=1
or
F_{\mathcal{S}}(|\psi\rangle)\leq 1-\varepsilon
.

Output:

1
if
F_{\mathcal{S}}(|\psi\rangle)=1
and
0
if
F_{\mathcal{S}}(|\psi\rangle)\leq 1-\varepsilon
.

2

for

i=1

to

m

do

3

Measure the POVM
\{\Pi_{r}^{+},\mathbf{I}-\Pi_{r}^{+}\}
on
|\psi\rangle^{\otimes 2r}
and obtain the outcome
Z_{i}\in\{-1,1\}

4

5

return

1
if
Z_{i}=1
for all
i\in[m]
and
0
otherwise

Algorithm 5

Stabiliser testing algorithm from
[
GNW21
]

Theorem 75

(
[
GNW21
, Corollary 3.13]
)
.

Let
|\psi\rangle\in(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n}
,
\varepsilon>0
,
\delta\in(0,1)
, and
r\geq 2
an integer such that
\operatorname{gcd}(d,r)=1
.
Algorithm

5
always accepts if
F_{\mathcal{S}}(|\psi\rangle)=1
and rejects if
F_{\mathcal{S}}(|\psi\rangle)\leq 1-\varepsilon
with probability
\geq 1-\delta
. It uses
2r\lceil\frac{1}{C_{d,r}\varepsilon}\ln\frac{1}{\delta}\rceil
copies of
|\psi\rangle
where
C_{d,r}\triangleq\frac{1}{2}\big(1-(1-\frac{1}{4d^{2}})^{r-1}\big)
.

Proof.

If
F_{\mathcal{S}}(|\psi\rangle)=1
, then measuring the POVM
\{\Pi_{r}^{+},\mathbf{I}-\Pi_{r}^{+}\}
always returns
1
and
Algorithm

5
always succeeds (it is perfectly complete). If
F_{\mathcal{S}}(|\psi\rangle)\leq 1-\varepsilon
, then according to
Lemma

74
, the expected outcome
G_{r}(|\psi\rangle)
of measuring the POVM
\{\Pi_{r}^{+},\mathbf{I}-\Pi_{r}^{+}\}
is
G_{r}(|\psi\rangle)\leq 1-2C_{d,r}\varepsilon
. Then failure probability equals the probability that all
m=\lceil\frac{1}{C_{d,r}\varepsilon}\ln\frac{1}{\delta}\rceil
outcomes equal
1
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1+G_{r}(|\psi\rangle)}{2}\right)^{m}\leq(1-C_{d,r}\varepsilon)^{m}\leq\mathrm{e}^{-mC_{d,r}\varepsilon}\leq\delta.

Finally, each measurement requires
2r
copies of
|\psi\rangle
.
∎

Remark 76
.

For
r=O(d^{2})
,
C_{d,r}=\Theta(\frac{r}{d^{2}})
and
Algorithm

5
requires
O(\frac{d^{2}}{\varepsilon}\ln\frac{1}{\delta})
copies of
|\psi\rangle
.

We now develop an alternative for the above testing procedure: obtain
\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n\times 4}
through skewed Bell difference sampling and measure
\frac{1}{2}\bigotimes_{i=1}^{4}(\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{X}_{i}}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{X}_{i}}^{\dagger})+\frac{1}{2}\bigotimes_{i=1}^{4}(\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{X}_{i}}^{\dagger}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{X}_{i}})
on
|\psi\rangle^{\otimes 8}
. We now study the average outcome of such procedure, i.e.,

\displaystyle A(|\psi\rangle)

\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{1}{2}\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{X}\sim b_{\psi}}\left[\bigotimes_{i=1}^{4}\langle\psi|^{\otimes 2}\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{X}_{i}}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{X}_{i}}^{\dagger}|\psi\rangle^{\otimes 2}+\bigotimes_{i=1}^{4}\langle\psi|^{\otimes 2}\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{X}_{i}}^{\dagger}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{X}_{i}}|\psi\rangle^{\otimes 2}\right]

\displaystyle=\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{X}\sim b_{\psi}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{4}|\langle\psi|\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{X}_{i}}|\psi\rangle|^{2}\right]=d^{4n}\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{X}\sim b_{\psi}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{4}p_{\psi}(\mathbf{X}_{i})\right].

Remark 77
.

Note that
\frac{1}{2}\bigotimes_{i=1}^{4}(\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{X}_{i}}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{X}_{i}}^{\dagger})+\frac{1}{2}\bigotimes_{i=1}^{4}(\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{X}_{i}}^{\dagger}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{X}_{i}})
can be performed transversally and takes
O(n)
time.

Lemma 78
.

For any
|\psi\rangle\in(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n}
,

\displaystyle A(|\psi\rangle)

\displaystyle=d^{8n}\sum_{\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n\times 4}}\prod_{i=1}^{4}p_{\psi}(\mathbf{X}_{i})^{2}p_{\psi}((\mathbf{X}\mathbf{R})_{i}).

Proof.

\displaystyle A(|\psi\rangle)

\displaystyle=d^{4n}\sum_{\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n\times 4}}b_{\psi}(\mathbf{X})\prod_{i=1}^{4}p_{\psi}(\mathbf{X}_{i})

\displaystyle=d^{4n}\sum_{\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n\times 4}}\prod_{i=1}^{4}p_{\psi}(\mathbf{X}_{i})p_{\psi}(\mathbf{X}_{i}+\mathbf{Y}_{i})p_{\psi}((\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{R})_{i})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d^{4n}}\sum_{\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z},\mathbf{Z}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n\times 4}}\prod_{i=1}^{4}p_{\psi}(\mathbf{X}_{i})\omega^{[\mathbf{X}_{i}+\mathbf{Y}_{i},\mathbf{Z}_{i}]}p_{\psi}(\mathbf{Z}_{i})\omega^{[(\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{R})_{i},(\mathbf{Z}^{\prime}\mathbf{R})_{i}]}p_{\psi}((\mathbf{Z}^{\prime}\mathbf{R})_{i})

(Fourier expansion and
Lemma

15
)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d^{4n}}\sum_{\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z},\mathbf{Z}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n\times 4}}\prod_{i=1}^{4}p_{\psi}(\mathbf{X}_{i})\omega^{[\mathbf{X}_{i}+\mathbf{Y}_{i},\mathbf{Z}_{i}]}p_{\psi}(\mathbf{Z}_{i})\omega^{-[\mathbf{Y}_{i},\mathbf{Z}^{\prime}_{i}]}p_{\psi}((\mathbf{Z}^{\prime}\mathbf{R})_{i})

(
\mathbf{R}\mathbf{R}^{\top}=(D-1)\mathbf{I}
)

\displaystyle=d^{4n}\sum_{\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n\times 4}}\prod_{i=1}^{4}p_{\psi}(\mathbf{X}_{i})\omega^{[\mathbf{X}_{i},\mathbf{Z}_{i}]}p_{\psi}(\mathbf{Z}_{i})p_{\psi}((\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{R})_{i})

(by
Lemma

9
)

\displaystyle=d^{8n}\sum_{\mathbf{Z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n\times 4}}\prod_{i=1}^{4}p_{\psi}(\mathbf{Z}_{i})^{2}p_{\psi}((\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{R})_{i}).

(Fourier expansion and
Lemma

15
)  ∎

Similarly to
Lemma

74
, we can relate
A_{\psi}
with the stabiliser fidelity
F_{\mathcal{S}}(|\psi\rangle)
.

Lemma 79
.

Let
C_{d,3}\triangleq\frac{1}{2}\big(1-(1-\frac{1}{4d^{2}})^{2}\big)
. For all
|\psi\rangle\in(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n}
,

\displaystyle A(|\psi\rangle)\leq G_{3}(|\psi\rangle)^{4}\leq\big(1-2C_{d,3}(1-F_{\mathcal{S}}(|\psi\rangle))\big)^{4}.

Proof.

\displaystyle A(|\psi\rangle)

\displaystyle=d^{8n}\sum_{\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n\times 4}}\prod_{i=1}^{4}p_{\psi}(\mathbf{X}_{i})^{2}p_{\psi}((\mathbf{X}\mathbf{R})_{i})

(by
Lemma

78
)

\displaystyle\leq d^{8n}\sum_{\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n\times 4}}\prod_{i=1}^{4}p_{\psi}(\mathbf{X}_{i})^{3}

\displaystyle=\left(d^{2n}\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n}}p_{\psi}(\mathbf{x})^{3}\right)^{4}

\displaystyle\leq\big(1-2C_{d,3}(1-F_{\mathcal{S}}(|\psi\rangle))\big)^{4}.

(by
Equations

6
and
74
)  ∎

By measuring
\frac{1}{2}\bigotimes_{i=1}^{4}(\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{X}_{i}}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{X}_{i}}^{\dagger})+\frac{1}{2}\bigotimes_{i=1}^{4}(\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{X}_{i}}^{\dagger}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{X}_{i}})
on
|\psi\rangle^{\otimes 8}
with
\mathbf{X}\sim b_{\psi}
, we develop a different algorithm for testing stabiliser states.

1

Input:

16m+8
copies of
|\psi\rangle\in(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n}
where
m\triangleq\lceil\big(2+\frac{1}{4C_{d,3}\varepsilon}\big)\ln\frac{1}{\delta}\rceil
and
C_{d,3}\triangleq\frac{1}{2}\big(1-(1-\frac{1}{4d^{2}})^{2}\big)
.

Promise:

either
F_{\mathcal{S}}(|\psi\rangle)=1
or
F_{\mathcal{S}}(|\psi\rangle)\leq 1-\varepsilon
.

Output:

1
if
F_{\mathcal{S}}(|\psi\rangle)=1
and
0
if
F_{\mathcal{S}}(|\psi\rangle)\leq 1-\varepsilon
.

2

for

i\leftarrow 0

to

m

do

3

Perform Bell sampling on
(\mathbf{I}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{R}}^{\dagger})|\psi\rangle^{\otimes 8}
to obtain
\mathbf{X}^{(i)}\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{2n\times 4}

4

if

i\neq 0

then

5

\mathbf{X}^{(i)}\leftarrow\mathbf{X}^{(i)}-\mathbf{X}^{(0)}

6

Measure
\frac{1}{2}\bigotimes_{j=1}^{4}(\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{X}_{j}^{(i)}}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{X}_{j}^{(i)}}^{\dagger})+\frac{1}{2}\bigotimes_{j=1}^{4}(\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{X}_{j}^{(i)}}^{\dagger}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{X}_{j}^{(i)}})
on
|\psi\rangle^{\otimes 8}
to obtain
Z_{i}\in[-1,1]

7

return

1
if
Z_{i}=1
for all
i\in[m]
and
0
otherwise

Algorithm 6

Alternative stabiliser testing algorithm

Theorem 80
.

Let
|\psi\rangle\in(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n}
,
\varepsilon>0
, and
\delta\in(0,1)
.
Algorithm

6
always accepts if
F_{\mathcal{S}}(|\psi\rangle)=1
and rejects if
F_{\mathcal{S}}(|\psi\rangle)\leq 1-\varepsilon
with probability at least
1-\delta
. It uses
16\lceil\big(2+\frac{1}{4C_{d,3}\varepsilon}\big)\ln\frac{1}{\delta}\rceil+8
copies of
|\psi\rangle
, where
C_{d,3}\triangleq\frac{1}{2}\big(1-(1-\frac{1}{4d^{2}})^{2}\big)
.

Proof.

Let
A(|\psi\rangle)\in[-1,1]
be the average outcome of measuring
\frac{1}{2}\bigotimes_{i=1}^{4}(\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{X}_{i}}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{X}_{i}}^{\dagger})+\frac{1}{2}\bigotimes_{i=1}^{4}(\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{X}_{i}}^{\dagger}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{X}_{i}})
on
|\psi\rangle^{\otimes 8}
with
\mathbf{X}\sim b_{\psi}
.
If
F_{\mathcal{S}}(|\psi\rangle)=1
, then
A(|\psi\rangle)=1
and
Algorithm

6
always succeeds (it is perfectly complete). If
F_{\mathcal{S}}(|\psi\rangle)\leq 1-\varepsilon
, then according to
Lemma

79
,
A(|\psi\rangle)\leq(1-2C_{d,3}\varepsilon)^{4}
. Note that we can, without loss of generality, shift the measurement outcomes by
1
. That means that the new measurement outcomes satisfy
Z_{i}\in[0,2]
, while their expected value becomes
1+A(|\psi\rangle)\leq 1+(1-2C_{d,3}\varepsilon)^{4}
. By Markov’s inequality,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Pr}[Z_{i}\geq 2]\leq\frac{1+A(|\psi\rangle)}{2}\leq\frac{1+(1-2C_{d,3}\varepsilon)^{4}}{2}.

The failure probability of
Algorithm

6
equals the probability that all
m=\lceil\big(2+\frac{1}{4C_{d,3}\varepsilon}\big)\ln\frac{1}{\delta}\rceil
outcomes
Z_{i}
equal
2
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1+(1-2C_{d,3}\varepsilon)^{4}}{2}\right)^{m}\leq\left(\frac{1+\frac{1}{1+8C_{d,3}\varepsilon}}{2}\right)^{m}=\left(\frac{1+4C_{d,3}\varepsilon}{1+8C_{d,3}\varepsilon}\right)^{m}\leq\exp\left(-m\frac{4C_{d,3}\varepsilon}{1+8C_{d,3}\varepsilon}\right)\leq\delta.

Finally, we need
16m+8=16\lceil\big(2+\frac{1}{4C_{d,3}\varepsilon}\big)\ln\frac{1}{\delta}\rceil+8
copies of
|\psi\rangle\in(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n}
.
∎

Remark 81
.

For small enough
\varepsilon
or large enough
d
,
Algorithm

6
uses
\approx\frac{4}{C_{d,3}\varepsilon}\ln\frac{1}{\delta}
copies of
|\psi\rangle
, which is less than
Algorithm

5
for
r=3
, i.e.,
\approx\frac{6}{C_{d,3}\varepsilon}\ln\frac{1}{\delta}
.

9.1
Tolerant stabiliser testing

Having worked with the “vanilla” testing problem of whether
F_{\mathcal{S}}(|\psi\rangle)=1
or
F_{\mathcal{S}}(|\psi\rangle)\leq 1-\varepsilon
, we now focus on its tolerant version where one wants to test whether
F_{\mathcal{S}}(|\psi\rangle)\geq 1-\varepsilon_{1}
or
F_{\mathcal{S}}(|\psi\rangle)\leq 1-\varepsilon_{2}
for
\varepsilon_{1}<\varepsilon_{2}
. A tolerant stabiliser testing procedure for qubits was proposed by Grewal et al.
[
GIKL23b
]
which requires
O(1/\gamma^{2})
copies of
|\psi\rangle
, where
\gamma\triangleq(1-\epsilon_{1})^{6}-\frac{4-3\varepsilon_{2}}{4}
. Their algorithm thus works as long as
(1-\varepsilon_{1})^{6}>\frac{4-3\varepsilon_{2}}{4}
